The text from "The Routledge Handbook of Second Language Acquisition and Neurolinguistics" focuses on memory consolidation in second language neurocognition. It introduces the concept of declarative (explicit) and non-declarative (implicit) memory systems, explaining how new experiences, like learning a novel word such as "mushrim," are initially encoded as episodic memories before potentially becoming part of semantic memory over time.

Key structures involved in this process include the hippocampus, which is crucial for retaining episodic memories. Although patients with hippocampal damage struggle to form new episodic memories or learn new words, they can still engage in verbal communication. This has led researchers to propose that memory consolidation involves dynamic shifts of memory traces from the hippocampus to other brain areas like the left temporal cortex, where lexical representations are stored post-consolidation.

Memory consolidation makes these traces more resistant to forgetting and involves changes such as integration into existing semantic networks. According to the complementary learning systems (CLS) model, new memories are initially kept separate from established knowledge in the neocortex to prevent interference but can gradually integrate through interleaved learning. 

The text also discusses lexicalization, where a newly learned word becomes part of the broader lexical network, transitioning from an episodic trace to a semantic representation. Methods like the lexical decision task and semantically primed lexical decision tasks are used in research to study how new words interact with existing ones, aiding our understanding of memory consolidation processes in language acquisition.

The text discusses key concepts in neurolinguistics related to second language acquisition and memory consolidation processes. Here are the main ideas:

1. **Pause Detection Task**: This task involves participants identifying artificially inserted pauses within spoken words. Reaction times (RTs) vary with lexical neighborhood density—the more phonologically similar words a target word has, the slower the RT.

2. **ERP Components**:
   - **N400**: An event-related potential linked to semantic processing in word recognition, reflecting automatic aspects of semantic retrieval.
   - **LPC (Late Positive Component)**: Another ERP component that relates to more controlled semantic retrieval processes compared to N400.

3. **Prime Lexicality Effect**: This effect occurs when a masked prime and target word are orthographically related. Reaction times improve in lexical decisions if the prime is a nonword, but not if it's a word. This phenomenon has been observed in first language (L1) but not consistently in second language (L2).

4. **Memory Consolidation**: The process involves stabilizing novel words through interaction with similar words in the lexicon once they are integrated into semantic memory. Sleep and time are crucial for promoting this consolidation, as they help stabilize important memories.

5. **Theoretical Models**:
   - **CLS Model**: It emphasizes how episodic memories become part of the mental lexicon after consolidation.
   - **Declarative/Procedural Model (Ullman)**: Differentiates between procedural learning in early language acquisition and declarative learning common in adult L2 acquisition, highlighting interactions between these memory systems.
   - **Processing-Based Model (Henke)**: Proposes rapid encoding through hippocampal-neocortical networks versus slow repetition-based learning via basal ganglia-cerebellum circuits.

6. **L1 Lexicalization**: Refers to the process where new words integrate into an existing lexicon, typically measured using tasks like lexical decision and pause detection. Consolidation is necessary for this integration.

These concepts contribute to understanding how language acquisition and memory consolidation interplay in both first and second languages.

The text from "The Routledge Handbook of Second Language Acquisition and Neurolinguistics" discusses how second language acquisition and lexicalization are influenced by various factors such as learning tasks, sleep, and brain activity. Here's a summary focusing on the main ideas:

1. **Lexicalization Process**: Lexicalization refers to the integration of new words into a learner’s mental lexicon. The text highlights that after participants learn a novel word (like "cathedruke"), their ability to process related tasks (such as determining if "cathedral" has an artificial pause) becomes faster following lexicalization. Initially, this effect is not observed until the word is fully integrated.

2. **Role of Sleep**: While sleep appears to support lexicalization, it is not absolutely necessary for this process. Learning can occur through interleaved sessions without sleep, and some cases like fast-mapping allow memory acquisition with minimal repetition or sleep.

3. **Lexical Configuration vs. Engagement**: Words first achieve a state called "lexical configuration," where they are recognized by definition or pronunciation. Once words start interacting with other lexicon items (e.g., showing priming effects), they reach "lexical engagement," equivalent to full lexicalization.

4. **Neurocognitive Evidence**:
   - **EEG/MEG Studies**: These studies use brainwave patterns, such as the N400 and LPC components in EEG, to detect lexicalization. The findings suggest that both newly learned words and existing ones show similar ERP patterns once lexicalized.
   - **Behavioral Results**: They also indicate that sleep is not required for lexicalization to occur.

5. **fMRI Findings**: Functional MRI studies support the idea of a shift in word representation from episodic memory regions like the hippocampus to more distributed neocortical networks, such as the pMTG, after consolidation.

Overall, the text emphasizes the dynamic and multifaceted nature of second language lexical acquisition, incorporating cognitive processes and neurobiological mechanisms.

The text from "The Routledge Handbook of Second Language Acquisition and Neurolinguistics" discusses studies related to neural processes involved in word learning, both in first (L1) and second languages (L2), focusing on memory consolidation.

### Main Ideas:

1. **Neural Structures and Word Learning**:
   - Studies investigated how the brain's neural structures support lexicalization during word learning.
   - Findings from fMRI studies (e.g., Takashima et al., 2014; Landi et al., 2018) show that both medial temporal regions and neocortical areas are active after learning. The hippocampus remains involved in maintaining new word representations, even after short consolidation periods.
   - The time course of consolidation may vary depending on whether words are learned with associated meanings.

2. **Comparison Between L1 and L2 Lexicalization**:
   - Researchers explore whether lexicalization processes differ between first language (L1) and second language (L2) learning.
   - Lindsay and Gaskell (2010) suggest no fundamental difference in the principles of L1 and L2 lexicalization, though L2 learning often benefits from prior knowledge of word meanings.

3. **Behavioral Evidence for Lexicalization**:
   - Behavioral studies indicate that newly learned L2 words exhibit signs of lexicalization similar to L1.
   - For instance, a pause detection task showed that English pseudowords learned two days prior were lexicalized (Tartaro et al., 2021).
   - However, evidence from semantic priming tasks is mixed. Some studies found L2 lexicalization comparable to L1, while others did not observe the same effects.

4. **Challenges in Understanding Lexicalization**:
   - There is no consensus on whether word-form and word-meaning level lexicalizations are parallel.
   - The findings suggest that the process might depend on whether new words belong to a new or existing second language.

Overall, these studies highlight the complexities of neural and behavioral processes involved in L1 and L2 learning, suggesting both shared mechanisms and unique challenges.

The text provides an overview of studies investigating lexicalization in second language (L2) word learning, focusing on memory consolidation and neurocognitive processes. Here are the main ideas from each study:

1. **Tartaro et al., 2021**: This study involved Italian–English late sequential bilinguals who learned novel English-like words for known objects. The findings showed that participants detected pauses slower for words learned two days before testing (remote) compared to those learned on the same day (recent). In a primed lexical decision task, both remote and recent new words facilitated response times. Additionally, in a recognition task, both remote and recent words increased hippocampal activity more than untrained words, with no difference observed between them in the posterior middle temporal gyrus (pMTG).

2. **Bakker-Marshall et al., 2021**: This study compared English monolinguals and early Spanish-English bilinguals learning Swahili–English word pairs. Monolinguals showed a priming effect for words learned one day before testing (remote) in a lexical decision task with primes being the learned Swahili words. However, there was no difference in brain activity between remote and recent words during recognition tasks.

3. **Havas et al., 2018**: Spanish monolinguals learned Hungarian words and were tested after either having slept overnight (sleep + group) or not (sleep – group). The study found no priming effect of L2 words for either group in a semantic priming task, regardless of the presence of sleep-associated consolidation.

Overall, these studies explore how different factors such as timing, bilingual experience, and sleep affect lexicalization processes and memory consolidation in L2 learning.

The text from "The Routledge Handbook of Second Language Acquisition and Neurolinguistics" explores how second language (L2) lexicalization parallels first language (L1) learning through the Consolidation-Lexicalization-Semanticization (CLS) model, highlighting various studies that support this connection. 1. **Priming Effects**: Nonword primes facilitate responses in both L1 and L2 learners, whereas word primes can be inhibitory. However, phonological influences remain less explored, indicating a potential research area in modality effects within language learning.

4. **Learning Settings**: Differences exist between explicit (classroom) and implicit (immersion) L2 learning environments, affecting the processing of new words as shown by ERP studies. Exploring how these settings influence lexicalization could enhance understanding of adult language acquisition.

5. **Individual Variability**: Investigating individual differences in L2 lexicalization is an emerging research direction. Factors like exposure type and personal language learning history may significantly affect how individuals consolidate and semantically integrate new vocabulary.

The chapter explores the impact of various learning environments—such as classrooms, immersion settings, or virtual reality—on L2 learning by considering factors like input quantity and quality, social support, motivation, and brain network activation.

Key theoretical perspectives examined include:

1. **The Declarative/Procedural Model**: Suggests that both first language (L1) and second language (L2) acquisition rely on declarative memory (DM), which stores facts and events, and procedural memory (PM), responsible for skills and habits.

The text discusses how different memory systems—Declarative Memory (DM) and Procedural Memory (PM)—play roles in second language acquisition, particularly in the context of adult learners acquiring a second language (L2). DM is associated with explicit knowledge that can be consciously accessed and quickly learned, while PM involves implicit knowledge learned more gradually. The reliance on either memory system for learning linguistic knowledge depends on factors such as the amount and type of input, instructional methods, and attention given to stimuli.

In L2 acquisition contexts, classroom settings often promote greater reliance on DM due to less input but more explicit instruction and conscious attention. In contrast, immersion environments tend to favor PM because they offer more input with less explicit instruction and reduced conscious focus on the language learning process.

The text references three longitudinal studies illustrating how these factors influence neurocognition in L2 grammar learning:

1. **Carpenter (2008)**: Investigated artificial language acquisition through different training conditions, showing that DM ability predicted performance for learners receiving explicit training, while PM ability was crucial at advanced proficiency in implicit conditions.

2. **Morgan-Short et al. (2015)**: Explored neural processing of syntax via fMRI, revealing that DM and PM abilities correlated with neural representations of grammar at different learning stages.

3. **Faretta-Stutenberg and Morgan-Short (2018)**: Examined L2 learners in classroom versus study-abroad settings, finding that for immersion-like contexts, PM predicted improvements in behavioral performance and changes in neurocognitive processing.

Overall, these studies suggest that individual cognitive abilities, particularly related to DM and PM, interact with learning context to influence language acquisition outcomes. The findings highlight the varying roles of DM and PM across different instructional environments and proficiency levels in L2 grammar learning.

The text from "The Routledge Handbook of Second Language Acquisition and Neurolinguistics" discusses the influence of learning contexts on second language (L2) neurocognition through various theoretical perspectives.

1. **Declarative/Procedural Model**: This model suggests that the roles of declarative memory (DM) and procedural memory (PM) in L2 acquisition are influenced by the context of learning, such as immersion settings. While laboratory studies provide controlled environments to explore these relationships, evidence from naturalistic settings is necessary for robust conclusions. A recent review highlights the need for research that investigates both explicit/instructed and implicit/uninstructed contexts to understand how they interact with DM/PM abilities.

2. **Generative Perspectives**: Generative approaches in SLA focus on describing L2 knowledge systems, including Universal Grammar, first language (L1) knowledge, and input from the second language (L2). These perspectives emphasize that while input is crucial for L2 acquisition—driving parameter resetting—it has limited effects compared to other factors. Learning context matters because it serves as a proxy for input quality and quantity. Generative theorists predict better outcomes in naturalistic environments like study abroad programs, though such predictions are not explicitly stated by them.

3. **Dynamic Restructuring Model**: This model explains how bilingualism affects neural function and structure through a non-linear process of neuroplasticity. It posits three stages—initial exposure, consolidation, peak efficiency—and corresponding brain restructuring processes: expansion, selection, renormalization. The context of L2 learning is crucial as it influences structural brain adaptations based on language use, environment, and timing of experiences.

Overall, the text underscores the importance of considering learning contexts in understanding L2 neurocognition across different theoretical models. Future research directions include more explicit predictions about how various contexts impact neural processing in bilingualism.

The text from "The Routledge Handbook of Second Language Acquisition and Neurolinguistics" discusses how different learning contexts influence second language (L2) neurocognition through brain adaptations. Key points include:

1. **Neural Effects and Brain Adaptations**: Exposure to a new language, particularly in immersive environments, leads to neural changes such as adaptations in subcortical structures like the cerebellum and caudate nucleus. These reflect improved control between languages due to increased learning demands.

2. **Dynamic Restructuring Model**: This model suggests that the type of brain restructuring varies across different stages of bilingualism: initial exposure, consolidation, and peak efficiency. Learning context influences these adaptations significantly. Studies by DeLuca et al. (2019) and Pliatsikas et al. (2017) show that immersion experiences lead to structural changes in the brain even without proficiency gains.

3. **Future Research Directions**: The Dynamic Restructuring Model encourages complex longitudinal study designs to capture non-linear changes in brain structure, acknowledging factors like language similarity and cultural differences as potential modulators of neurocognitive effects.

4. **Linguistic Relativity and L2 Learning**: Building on the Sapir-Whorf hypothesis, linguistic relativity posits that language shapes thought and perception. This theory extends to L2 learning, suggesting that exposure to a second language can influence non-verbal cognition through neuroplastic changes dependent on factors such as method, duration, and rate of exposure.

5. **Empirical Evidence**: Neurocognitive studies focus on non-verbal processing to explore linguistic relativity in L2 contexts, avoiding circularity by not directly assessing if the L2 is processed according to its distinctions but rather examining broader cognitive impacts. 

Overall, the text underscores the importance of learning context and immersion experiences in shaping brain adaptations during second language acquisition.

The text from "The Routledge Handbook of Second Language Acquisition and Neurolinguistics" explores the debate between classical amodal theories and embodied semantics in understanding language acquisition. Classical theories suggest that conceptual symbols are defined independently by other symbols, lacking real-world grounding. In contrast, embodied theories argue that these symbols must relate to real-world sensorimotor experiences for effective understanding.

Key concepts of embodied semantics include the "correlational learning principle," which posits that co-occurring action-perception and meaning lead to shared neural firing patterns, strengthening semantic processing. This theory supports the idea that language is tied to bodily experiences ("What fires together, wires together"), exemplified by how words like "kick" become linked with associated physical actions.

Embodied learning emphasizes the role of physical movement in encoding linguistic information. Studies show that using congruent gestures or movements during learning enhances retention (the enactment effect), supporting embodied learning theories like Zwaan and Madden's theory of experiential traces, where environmental interactions form associations with linguistic labels.

Behavioral studies on both first (L1) and second languages (L2) reveal motor-semantic interactions. These findings demonstrate that motor and semantic processes share resources, leading to facilitation or inhibition effects depending on their compatibility. L2 studies suggest similar patterns of embodied processing as in L1, although these effects can be attenuated.

Overall, the text highlights a shift towards recognizing the importance of bodily experiences in language processing and learning, challenging traditional cognitive models that separate linguistic symbols from real-world grounding.

The text discusses how language proficiency influences embodied language processing, specifically through motor-semantic interactions. Research by Vukovic (2013) shows that high L2 proficiency affects reaction times in identifying translations of verbs based on body parts involved, suggesting more situated "real-life" usage and co-activation of neural substrates like the motor cortex.

Neurocognitive studies using fMRI have found that both first language (L1) and second language (L2) processing engage similar neural networks for action verb comprehension. For example, Hauk et al. (2004) showed specific brain areas activated during passive reading of verbs related to bodily actions. Further fMRI research indicates that L2 speakers also exhibit embodied semantic representations but differ in the extent of cortical network engagement compared to L1 speakers.

Electroencephalography (EEG), with its superior temporal resolution, provides insights into early motor-semantic interactions. Studies have shown how linguistic processing can interfere with or enhance motor processes and vice versa. These findings suggest a bidirectional relationship between language comprehension and motor functions.

L2 proficiency plays a crucial role in embodied language effects. High proficiency L2 speakers show greater integration of sensory and linguistic information, as evidenced by EEG studies examining semantic congruency and motor preparation during tasks. Conversely, low proficiency speakers do not exhibit the same neural activations or connectivity patterns. Research also suggests that age of acquisition correlates with these embodied processing capabilities.

Overall, while both L1 and L2 can involve shared neural resources for motor and semantic processes, L2 proficiency significantly influences the strength and reliability of these effects.

The text from "The Routledge Handbook of Second Language Acquisition and Neurolinguistics" discusses the relationship between motor processes and language processing in both first (L1) and second languages (L2). Here's a summary focusing on the main ideas:

1. **Motor Activation in Language Processing**: Time-frequency analysis reveals that the suppression of certain brain rhythms is linked to activity in the sensorimotor cortex, which is involved in movement observation, imagery, and execution. This motor resonance occurs during language processing for action-related words.

2. **Differences Between L1 and L2**: Studies show that motor activation is present when processing both concrete (action) and abstract language in L1. However, this effect tends to be weaker in L2 compared to L1, although similar embodied effects are observed across both languages. The extent of these effects often depends on proficiency in the L2.

3. **Embodied Language Learning**: There is behavioral evidence that learning words with associated actions or gestures enhances memory retention and semantic encoding, supporting embodied semantics theories. This advantage appears tied not just to motor activation but also to the meaningful connection between gesture and word meaning.

4. **Role of Gesture in Learning**: While performing congruent gestures during language learning can improve short- and long-term retention of new vocabulary, simply observing these gestures may also activate sensorimotor systems beneficially. However, some studies suggest that spontaneously produced gestures might offer more significant benefits than conscious imitation, likely due to a deeper engagement with the motor system.

5. **Implications for Language Acquisition**: The research highlights the importance of considering proficiency and possibly age of acquisition when examining how embodied language effects manifest in L2 learning. Understanding these dynamics could influence approaches to teaching new languages by integrating physical actions or gestures into the learning process. Further studies are needed to clarify the conditions under which motor involvement most effectively enhances language learning.

The chapter from "The Routledge Handbook of Second Language Acquisition and Neurolinguistics" focuses on how native listeners perceive foreign-accented speech, which deviates from native pronunciation in a given language due to non-native speakers' challenges in achieving full proficiency. The interaction with such speech introduces complexities at phonological, lexical-semantic, and syntactic/morphosyntactic levels.

Key aspects discussed include:

1. **Phonological Challenges**: Native listeners must adapt their perception to novel sounds that differ from expected native pronunciations.
2. **Lexical-Semantic Challenges**: Listeners need to derive intended meanings when non-native speakers use less fitting words, often requiring multiple interpretations.
3. **Syntactic/Morphosyntactic Challenges**: Maintaining grammatical coherence in the face of imprecisions demands error correction by listeners.

The chapter delves into semantic and syntactic challenges specifically, exploring theoretical perspectives that explain differences in processing foreign- versus native-accented speech:

- **Cognitive Load Theory** suggests that processing non-native speech requires more cognitive resources due to increased complexity.
  
- A second framework considers the probabilistic knowledge linked with stereotypes of foreign speakers' language production.

- The third perspective focuses on how phonological analysis of foreign accents affects higher levels of language processing, creating cascading effects.

Research methods discussed include behavioral studies, electrophysiological measures, and neuroimaging techniques to understand these processes.

The text from "The Routledge Handbook of Second Language Acquisition and Neurolinguistics" discusses several key theories and findings related to processing foreign-accented speech:

1. **Cognitive Load Theory**: This theory suggests that tasks with higher cognitive demands, such as understanding foreign-accented speech, can lead to decreased performance and retention due to increased cognitive load.

2. **Cognitive Effort Theory**: Contrasting with Cognitive Load Theory, this posits that higher cognitive effort may enhance task performance and information retention through the reallocation of attentional resources.

3. **Accent Perception and Stereotypical Knowledge**: Listeners quickly form stereotypes about speakers based on accents, incorporating expectations during language processing. This stereotypical knowledge is learned from recurring patterns in foreign-accented speech across phonological, semantic, and syntactic levels.

4. **Probabilistic Learning**: Listeners learn the distribution of characteristics within accented speech, which influences their perception and interaction with such inputs. The degree of exposure to an accent affects how it is processed, and typical versus atypical errors in foreign-accented speech are perceived differently based on learned frequency distributions.

5. **Phonological Challenges**: Processing foreign accents involves dealing with acoustic divergences from native phonological categories. A normalization process during word recognition attempts to align these divergent sounds with native representations, but this is less effective for full sentences, leading to cascading issues in comprehension at higher cognitive levels.

6. **Research Outcomes**: Studies often use violation paradigms comparing incorrect and correct linguistic conditions to understand how foreign-accented speech affects semantic and syntactic processing. These studies provide insights into the behavioral and neural responses associated with accent-related differences in speech perception. 

Overall, these theories and findings highlight the complexity of processing foreign-accented speech and the interplay between cognitive load, effort, stereotypical knowledge, and phonological challenges.

The text from "The Routledge Handbook of Second Language Acquisition and Neurolinguistics" explores how listeners process foreign-accented speech using the violation paradigm. The main points are:

1. **Behavioral Findings**: 
   - Listeners tend to take longer (longer reaction times) to process semantically when encountering foreign accents, though their comprehension accuracy remains largely equivalent for both native and foreign accents.
   - Contextual information helps listeners better comprehend foreign-accented speech by supplementing potentially unclear acoustic input.
   - Non-native speakers are often perceived as less credible but are not significantly penalized for speech disfluencies or content omissions.

2. **Syntactic Processing**: 
   - Listeners can identify grammatical errors in foreign-accented speech, albeit with slightly reduced accuracy compared to native-accented speech.
   - Comprehension of both native and foreign-accented sentences is generally high, even when subtler morphosyntactic errors are present.

3. **Electrophysiological Findings**:
   - Electroencephalography (EEG) helps study brain responses to linguistic events. Event-related potentials (ERPs) such as the N400 are used to assess semantic processing.
   - The findings on semantics show mixed results regarding foreign accents: some studies find no influence, others report increased cognitive demands for integrating meaning in non-native speech.

Overall, while there are accent-related differences in processing times and perceived credibility, listeners generally achieve accurate comprehension of both native and foreign-accented speech. Behavioral effects tend to be small, aligning with Cognitive Load Theory.

The text discusses various neurocognitive components involved in language processing as observed through Event-Related Potentials (ERPs). Here are the main ideas:

1. **N400**: 
   - This ERP component is associated with lexical-semantic access and analysis.
   - It typically manifests as a greater negative amplitude when there are semantic violations compared to correct controls.
   - The N400 effect has been observed in studies involving language processing, showing different responses based on the type of accent or manipulation involved.

2. **Nref**:
   - Related to pronoun resolution and referential processing difficulty.
   - It shows increased negativity during errors in retrieving the appropriate antecedent for a pronoun.

3. **P600**:
   - Associated with syntactic and semantic violations, including word order and grammatical agreement issues.
   - Thought to relate to processes of repair and analysis aimed at overcoming errors to comprehend sentence meaning fully.
   - Characterized by an increased positive amplitude in response to violated conditions compared to correct ones.

4. **Foreign Accent Perception**:
   - Various studies are summarized regarding how different accents (e.g., native, foreign) affect semantic processing as measured by ERPs like the N400.
   - Different manipulations and languages have been studied, showing varied ERP responses such as reduced or similar N400 effects based on accent familiarity.

These components highlight how specific brain responses correlate with language processing tasks involving semantic and syntactic comprehension across different accents.

The text discusses research on how the brain processes language with different accents using Event-Related Potentials (ERPs), particularly focusing on the N400 response. The studies mentioned involve bilingual or multilingual participants who are exposed to native, regional, and foreign-accented speech under various conditions, such as semantic violations (OSV), subtle violations (SSV), and anomalies (SA).

Key findings across these studies include:

1. **N400 Response**: This component is associated with the processing of meaning in language. Both native and foreign accents can elicit similar N400 responses under certain conditions, but there are variations depending on the type of semantic violation and accent familiarity.
   
2. **Accent Familiarity Effects**: Studies like Holt et al., 2018 show that participants familiar with a particular accent may exhibit larger N400 effects for sentences spoken in that accent.

3. **Cognitive Load**: The Cognitive Load Theory suggests that different types of semantic manipulations require varying levels of cognitive effort. Outright violations are more demanding, potentially depleting resources and affecting the ability to detect accent differences (Grey et al., 2019; Romero-Rivas et al., 2015).

4. **Cognitive Effort Theory**: Suggests that when less cognitive effort is needed for processing, listeners might better distinguish foreign accents, showing more pronounced effects in favor of foreign-accented speech under certain conditions (Kędzierska, 2019; Strauber et al., 2021).

The studies highlight how methodological differences and theoretical frameworks like Cognitive Load Theory are important for understanding how semantic manipulations impact ERP responses to accented speech.

The text discusses findings from studies on second language acquisition and neurolinguistics, specifically focusing on how listeners process foreign-accented speech.

1. **Semantic Processing**: The variety in results from accented-speech experiments aligns with both the Cognitive Load framework and the probabilistic knowledge theory. Semantic violations in foreign-accented speech, such as outright semantic errors, tend to show delayed or absent effects compared to more frequent phonemic mispronunciations. Interestingly, native listeners familiar with a particular accent showed larger N400 effects for these semantic violations, contrary to expectations from the probabilistic knowledge framework.

2. **Complexity in Theoretical Frameworks**: Neither the Cognitive Load framework nor the probabilistic knowledge perspective is conclusively supported by current literature. Studies indicate that phonological analysis influences higher-level semantic processing, suggesting a complex interaction between accent and comprehension.

3. **Syntax Processing**: Listeners adjust their processing of grammatical errors based on speaker identity (accent). They often show tolerance for typical foreign-accented errors, modifying their response strategies (e.g., reduced P600 effects) based on stereotypical knowledge about the accent. This suggests that listeners use probabilistic knowledge associated with speaker identities to parse non-native input.

4. **Electrophysiological Findings**: Studies consistently report a reduction in syntactic violation ERP effects (like P600) for foreign-accented speech, indicating adjusted processing strategies by native listeners who expect certain errors from specific accents.

Overall, the findings suggest that listener's knowledge about speaker identity and accent stereotypes significantly impact real-time language processing. Future research is encouraged to explore these complex interactions further.

The text from "The Routledge Handbook of Second Language Acquisition and Neurolinguistics" discusses the phenomenon known as the Foreign Language Effect (FLe), which describes how using a foreign language can influence decision-making processes such as economic choices and moral judgments. This effect was initially identified in a study by Keysar et al. (2012) that examined cognitive biases related to risk aversion. The study found that when decisions were made in a foreign language, the typical bias of being risk-averse for gains and risk-seeking for losses—often influenced by problem framing—was mitigated.

The FLe suggests that the use of a second language can alter decision-making behavior compared to using one's native language. This has been observed in various contexts, such as economic decisions (Costa et al., 2014) and moral judgments (Costa et al., 2014; Geipel et al., 2015). The text from "The Routledge Handbook of Second Language Acquisition and Neurolinguistics" explores how using a foreign language can influence decision-making processes. It highlights the phenomenon known as the Foreign Language Effect (FLE), which suggests that decisions made in a non-native language are less influenced by emotional biases and more rational compared to those made in one's native language.

The text provides examples of decision scenarios, such as moral dilemmas and cognitive tasks, where individuals show different responses when using their foreign language. For instance, in moral dilemmas like the "Footbridge" scenario (a personal moral dilemma) versus the "Trolley" scenario (an impersonal dilemma), people tend to make more rational, utilitarian decisions rather than emotionally driven, deontological ones when thinking in a foreign language.

This phenomenon is linked to dual-process theories of decision-making, which distinguish between two systems: System 1 (fast, intuitive, emotional) and System 2 (slow, rational, controlled). The FLE suggests that using a foreign language might promote the use of System 2 over System 1, leading to more deliberative decisions. However, it remains debated whether this shift is due to an increase in rational processing or a suppression of emotional responses.

The text also notes variability in research findings, which may be attributed to differences in study methodologies and tasks used. While some studies report reduced biases when using a foreign language, others do not find significant effects, especially in non-emotional cognitive tasks.

Overall, the FLE is a robust phenomenon observed across various decision types, but its underlying mechanisms are still under investigation, with theories suggesting it might involve either an enhancement of rational processing or a suppression of emotional responses.

The Foreign Language Effect (FLe) is often attributed to increased cognitive load and reduced emotionality in a foreign language. Factors like language proficiency, experience, and similarity influence the FLe, but results are mixed. Some studies indicate that higher proficiency diminishes the FLe, while others show minimal impact of self-reported proficiency on decision-making.

Investigations into high-proficiency bilinguals reveal varying outcomes—some showing no FLe differences in moral dilemmas, while others suggest language dominance plays a role. Early bilinguals exhibit changes in risk preferences depending on the language used, indicating that variables beyond proficiency, such as cultural influence and language similarity, might modulate the effect.

Recent meta-analyses confirm the FLe's robustness but disagree on the extent to which linguistic factors like proficiency or language similarity impact it. This suggests that while language variables contribute to the FLe, their exact role remains unclear.

The text explores cognitive control in second language (L2) acquisition, particularly focusing on late bilinguals—those who acquire a second language later in life. It highlights how cognitive control plays a crucial role in managing interference from the first language (L1) during L2 production. Key aspects include:

1. **Inhibitory Control Model**: This model suggests that bilinguals need to inhibit their native language's lexical items to successfully produce words in L2. This involves two phases: competition between language schemas and inhibition of non-target lexical items.

2. **Proactive vs. Reactive Control**: Proactive control adjusts activation levels before target word production, while reactive control resolves interference after it occurs.

3. **Language Proficiency and Inhibition**: The model posits that the stronger language requires more inhibition. Studies show balanced bilinguals exhibit similar inhibition across languages, whereas unbalanced bilinguals inhibit their dominant language more.

4. **Adaptive Control Hypothesis (ACH)**: This hypothesis suggests that bilinguals adjust their language control mechanisms based on context, such as code-switching or dual-language use, engaging different cognitive processes accordingly.

5. **Relationship with Domain-General Cognitive Control**: The text discusses the connection between bilingual language control and broader cognitive control systems, suggesting that these controls are interrelated and not confined to linguistic processing alone.

Overall, the chapter emphasizes the dynamic nature of bilingual language control and its reliance on both specific linguistic mechanisms and general cognitive processes.

The text from "The Routledge Handbook of Second Language Acquisition and Neurolinguistics" focuses on exploring the relationship between bilingual language control and domain-general cognitive control. Here are the main ideas summarized:

1. **Cognitive Control in Bilinguals**: Studies have shown that bilingual individuals use cognitive mechanisms, such as those managed by the prefrontal cortex, for controlling two languages. This involves inhibiting one language while using another.

2. **Neural Correlates**: Neuroimaging studies indicate overlapping brain regions involved in both bilingual language control and general cognitive processes like inhibition and task switching. Key areas include the left dorsolateral prefrontal cortex, anterior cingulate cortex, and cerebellum.

3. **Executive Functions**: Domain-general executive functions (inhibition, shifting, updating) play a role in managing multiple languages. Inhibitory control helps suppress non-target language activation; cognitive flexibility aids in switching between languages, while working memory updates maintain relevant linguistic information.

   - Studies using tasks such as the flanker task and cued language switching paradigms show correlations between domain-general abilities (like interference inhibition) and bilingual language control.
   - ERP studies highlight specific neural responses associated with these cognitive processes during language use.

5. **Conclusion**: The overlap of neural mechanisms in both language control and general cognitive functions suggests a shared resource model, indicating that bilinguals leverage common brain systems for managing languages and other complex cognitive tasks.

The text from "The Routledge Handbook of Second Language Acquisition and Neurolinguistics" discusses research on the neural mechanisms underlying bilingual language control and general cognitive control. Key findings include:

1. **Shared Neural Correlates**: Both bilingual language control and domain-general cognitive control activate similar brain regions, such as the left inferior frontal junction, medial prefrontal cortex (including dACC and preSMA), and parietal lobe areas.

2. **Differential Activation**: Despite shared areas, differences exist in how these regions are activated during linguistic versus non-linguistic tasks. For instance, language switching tends to engage the left prefrontal cortex more than task switching does with the right prefrontal cortex.

3. **Language-Specific Control Regions**: Some studies suggest that the left caudate might play a unique role in coordinating both language and non-linguistic control, although its specific involvement is debated.

4. **Brain Network Reconfiguration**: Recent evidence indicates that bilingual language control relies on a cooperative network involving frontal lobes, parietal cortex, subcortical areas, and the cerebellum. This network exhibits reconfigurable connectivity patterns, with particular reliance on connections from frontal to subcortical regions.

5. **Dynamic Adaptations**: Emerging research is focusing on dynamic changes in brain function during bilingual language production. Studies are exploring how functional changes within specific brain regions or training paradigms affect domain-general and language-specific control, indicating plasticity and adaptability in these processes.

Overall, while there is significant overlap between the neural mechanisms of bilingual language control and general cognitive control, ongoing research seeks to clarify distinct roles and dynamic adaptations in these systems.

The text discusses how bilingual children's brains process and recognize words in multiple languages. Key findings are:

1. **Neural Activation**: Both child and adult bilinguals show similar brain regions activated during word processing tasks in their first (L1) and second language (L2). However, L2 often requires more neural activation due to its lower familiarity.

2. **ERP Studies**: Event-Related Potential (ERP) studies indicate that known versus unknown word recognition processes are stronger in a bilingual's dominant language compared to their non-dominant one. This suggests that the dominance of a language affects cognitive processing efficiency.

3. **Developmental Differences**: Younger bilinguals exhibit both convergent and divergent neural responses for semantic relatedness, influenced by dual-language experience and proficiency. As they grow older, if they develop balanced bilingualism, their brain processes words similarly across languages.

4. **Language Membership Recognition**: In early balanced bilingual adults, the ability to recognize a word's language membership precedes categorizing its meaning. This suggests that language-specific neural processing becomes more distinct over time.

5. **Balanced vs. Non-Balanced Bilinguals**: Balanced bilinguals may process words across languages using similar brain regions, while non-balanced bilinguals might activate different networks depending on the language's dominance or familiarity.

6. **Overall Insights**: These findings highlight how early bilingual experiences shape word processing mechanisms and emphasize the importance of proficiency and language use patterns in determining neural activation during bilingualism. Further research is needed to understand early bilingual word learning fully.

The text also suggests that understanding these processes can enhance models explaining how bilingual infants and young children develop specific lexical abilities.

The text from "The Routledge Handbook of Second Language Acquisition and Neurolinguistics" discusses several key issues related to bilingual language acquisition, particularly focusing on how childhood exposure to a second language (L2) affects neural specialization for language processing.

1. **Neural Specialization in Bilinguals vs. Monolinguals**: The text explores whether the neural specialization for language in bilinguals is similar to that of monolinguals. Studies suggest that both groups follow a temporo-frontal trajectory in brain development, with children showing stronger responses in the temporal lobe and adults in the left inferior frontal gyrus (IFG).

2. **Neurodevelopmental Plasticity**: Bilingual children show greater engagement in the left hemisphere regions associated with language processing compared to monolinguals. This increased activation is attributed to neurodevelopmental plasticity, allowing bilinguals to efficiently process two languages.

Studies indicate that early-exposed bilinguals exhibit more adult-like neural patterns compared to monolingual children. Additionally, proficiency in the L2 affects neural responses, with high-proficiency bilinguals showing distinct activation patterns.

4. **Effects of Dual-Language Use**: Bilingual adults demonstrate neural processing related to linguistic proficiency and language use rather than general cognitive factors. Dominance effects are observed, with stronger neural responses in their dominant language.

5. **Morphosyntactic Knowledge Development**: The first 7–10 years of life are crucial for establishing morphosyntactic knowledge. Exposure to a second language during or after this period influences the neural pathways for syntactic and semantic processing.

Overall, the text suggests that childhood bilinguals develop specialized neural mechanisms for language processing, influenced by factors such as AoA, proficiency, and patterns of language use.

The text from "The Routledge Handbook of Second Language Acquisition and Neurolinguistics" explores various aspects of bilingual language processing:

1. **Semantic vs. Syntactic Processing**: Semantic open-class words (like nouns and verbs) are less affected by age of acquisition (AoA), whereas syntactic processing is influenced both by age and proficiency. Proficiency and use have more impact on semantic processing compared to AoA.

2. **Cross-Linguistic Influences**: Research indicates that bilingual individuals often experience cross-linguistic transfer, where one language influences the other. For instance:
   - In Spanish-Basque bilinguals, syntax processing shows different neural responses depending on language dominance.
   - Welsh-English bilinguals exhibit syntactic activation from their Welsh proficiency when processing English sentences with ungrammatical structures.

3. **Bilingual Neurodevelopment**: Bilingual and monolingual children generally follow similar neurodevelopmental patterns, but bilingualism introduces factors like AoA and proficiency that influence sentence comprehension. Cross-linguistic interactions can lead to transfer effects in both balanced and unbalanced bilinguals.

4. **Clinical Implications for "At-Risk" Learners**: There's a historical tendency to view dual language acquisition negatively, often recommending monolingual approaches for children with language impairments. However, research shows that bilingual processing is not inherently inefficient, and differences between bilinguals and monolinguals are more related to varied linguistic experiences.

5. **Future Directions in Research**: The field aims to better understand how the brain processes multiple languages simultaneously through multimodal neuroimaging techniques. This will help elucidate how language characteristics and dual-language use patterns influence neural processing.

Overall, the text emphasizes the plasticity of the developing brain in organizing dual-language representations and processing mechanisms.

The text from "The Routledge Handbook of Second Language Acquisition and Neurolinguistics" discusses various research studies related to second language (L2) acquisition with a focus on neurolinguistic perspectives. Here's a summary emphasizing the main ideas:

1. **Neurolinguistics in L2 Acquisition**:
   - The chapter focuses on the neurolinguistics of acquiring a second language, particularly through different modalities such as spoken and signed languages.
   - It reviews literature concerning how hearing adults learn a signed language when their native language is spoken (M2L2 learning).

2. **Modalities in Language Learning**:
   - Languages are categorized by modality: spoken languages use the vocal tract, while signed languages utilize visual-manual channels.
   - The majority of existing research focuses on M1L2 learners who acquire a second language within the same modality as their first.

3. **M2L2 Acquisition and Bimodal Bilingualism**:
   - M2L2 learning involves acquiring a language in a different modality from one's native language, such as a hearing person learning sign language.
   - This area is relatively underexplored but has significant theoretical and practical implications for understanding language universals and the role of modality-specific processes.

4. **Cultural and Neurolinguistic Context**:
   - Historically, signed languages were misinterpreted as inferior to spoken languages due to societal biases against deafness.
   - Recent recognition of signed languages like American Sign Language (ASL) as complete languages has shifted perspectives and increased research interest in their structure.

5. **Research Implications**:
   - Understanding M2L2 learning is crucial for developing instructional methods, especially as the number of learners in this category increases globally.
   - Studies explore how hearing individuals adapt their visual systems to recognize manual linguistic units and articulate them using hands, faces, and bodies.

The chapter underscores the importance of studying L2 acquisition across different modalities to enhance our understanding of language processing and support diverse learning needs.

The text discusses how neurocognitive research has established signed languages as natural languages by demonstrating similarities in brain processing between spoken and signed languages. Key findings include:

1. **ERP Studies**: Event-related potentials (ERPs) show that the brain's response to language variables like frequency, concreteness, and semantic similarity is similar across modalities.

2. **fMRI Findings**: Functional magnetic resonance imaging (fMRI) distinguishes between regions processing modality-specific perceptual information and those handling language irrespective of modality. The supramarginal gyrus (SMG) is highlighted as active in both spoken and signed language processing, suggesting its role in amodal phonological computations.

3. **Signed Language Learning**: Emphasizes the unique challenges in learning second languages in the visual-manual modality, particularly focusing on phonology and spatial grammar, which are distinct from spoken languages.

4. **Sublexical Phonology**: Signs consist of systematic combinations of sublexical units like handshapes, movements, and locations, analogous to phonemes in spoken language. Both signers and non-signers can perceive differences between similar signs, though the processing mechanisms differ.

5. **Neuroplasticity in Learners**: Longitudinal fMRI studies show that learning to process signs as phonological units develops over time. Early stages involve general motion processing regions, while later stages activate areas like the SMG and superior parietal lobe (SPL), associated with language-specific processing.

Overall, the research underscores the complexity of M2L2 (second language learning in a visual-manual modality) and parallels it with traditional second language acquisition principles.

The text explores how learners of a second language (L2) in the visual-manual modality, such as American Sign Language (ASL), develop phonological awareness and map form to meaning. It highlights two main areas:

1. **Phonological Learning and Errors**: Learners' errors in sign production can indicate their sensitivity to phonological structures. Research suggests movement is often the most challenging parameter for learners to master, both in comprehension and production. The text notes that hearing learners might simplify signs by using joints closer to the torso (proximalization), potentially as a strategy to manage motor demands. Despite improvements with proficiency, M2L2 learners continue to find certain parameters difficult.

2. **Iconicity and World Knowledge**: Iconicity, where sign forms resemble their meanings, aids comprehension but can hinder precise phonological learning. Learners access the meaning of iconic signs more quickly than non-iconic ones, benefiting from their resemblance to real-world objects or actions. However, this same iconicity might distract learners during production, as they may rely on familiar gestures instead of focusing on accurate sign forms. The concept of "manual cognates," where signed language signs overlap with co-speech gestures, illustrates how world knowledge supports learning by allowing predictions about new signs based on existing gestures.

Overall, the text underscores the complex interplay between phonological development and iconicity in M2L2 acquisition, highlighting both facilitative and challenging aspects.

The text from "The Routledge Handbook of Second Language Acquisition and Neurolinguistics" by Gabriela Meade focuses on two key areas in second language (M2L2) acquisition of signed languages: constructing sentences and narratives using space, and the role of non-manual markers.

1. **Spatial Referents**: M2L2 learners must learn to combine signs correctly while overcoming tendencies to directly translate from their first language (L1). Key skills include assigning referents to specific locations in signing space for later reference, which is essential for conveying relationships and actions using directional verbs. This spatial aspect supports more efficient communication but requires significant learning and cognitive development. Neuroimaging suggests the right parietal lobe's involvement in this process, although changes during learning are not fully understood.

2. **Development of Spatial Skills**: Research indicates that while M2L2 learners can initially use gestures to understand signed languages, systematic spatial usage develops over time. Learners often struggle with using established spatial locations consistently and making errors like "stacking" referents in space. However, improvements are seen after prolonged instruction.

3. **Non-manual Markers**: These involve body parts other than the hands (e.g., facial expressions) to convey syntactic information. M2L2 learners face challenges in coordinating these non-manual markers with manual signs. Overuse of mouthings from their L1 is a common issue as they attempt to differentiate meanings.

4. **Individual Differences**: While much emphasis has been placed on group performance, there is interest in understanding factors that contribute to individual success in M2L2 acquisition.

Overall, the text highlights the complexities involved in learning signed languages, emphasizing spatial and non-manual elements' cognitive demands and transfer from L1.

The text discusses the complexities involved in assessing and treating bilingual aphasia, a condition that affects individuals who speak two languages following acquired brain injury (ABI). Here are the main ideas:

1. **Heterogeneity of Bilinguals**: Bilingual individuals form a diverse group with different language profiles and proficiency levels, making it challenging to quantify impairments uniformly.

2. **Characterizing Aphasia in Bilinguals**: To diagnose aphasia accurately, it is crucial to consider pre-ABI language abilities in both the first (L1) and second languages (L2). This helps distinguish between unmastered aspects of a language prior to ABI and those impaired due to injury.

3. **Historical Perspectives on Language Loss**:
   - Early theories by Pitres and Ribot suggested asymmetrical impairment, with one language being more affected than the other.
   - Contemporary observations often show parallel impairments in both languages, attributed to shared neural substrates supporting L1 and L2.

4. **Patterns of Impairment**: 
   - Identified patterns include parallel (similar reduction in both languages), differential (one language more affected), and selective impairment (deficits in one language only).
   - Understanding these patterns requires assessing pre-ABI proficiency through language use questionnaires.

5. **Contemporary Approaches**:
   - Emphasize the need to quantify pre-morbid language abilities to understand post-ABI impairments better.
   - Consider dynamic changes in language proficiency and dominance over time due to life experiences, such as immigration or increased exposure to a second language environment.
   - Models like Gray and Kiran's propose that impairments in one domain can affect other linguistic domains, highlighting interconnectedness.

6. **Assessment Frameworks**: These models advocate for comprehensive evaluations of pre-ABI proficiency to predict post-ABI performance and guide effective rehabilitation strategies. 

Overall, the text underscores the importance of detailed assessments considering both historical and contemporary perspectives on bilingual aphasia to design effective interventions.

The text discusses research findings on bilingual aphasia, focusing on how pre-ABI (before acquired brain injury) language proficiency affects post-ABI performance and rehabilitation outcomes for bilingual individuals with aphasia (BPWA).

1. **Pre-ABI Language Proficiency**: Studies by Barrett & Kiran (2020) and Kuzmina et al. (2019) found that both L1 and L2 proficiencies prior to ABI are predictive of language abilities post-ABI, with a greater impact on L2. The age of acquisition (AoA) also plays a role, where early bilinguals show similar performance in both languages post-ABI, while late bilinguals perform better in their L1.

2. **Language Use and Recovery**: Greater pre-ABI use of a language correlates with better post-ABI performance in that language. Gil & Goral (2004) showed that rehabilitation outcomes can vary based on pre-ABI proficiency; a patient recovered more in L1 despite receiving treatment in L2, suggesting the importance of considering pre-ABI language use and preference.

3. **Language Control**: New evidence distinguishes between deficits in language control and overt language loss in BPWA. Language control issues manifest as pathological switching and mixing of languages, often due to lesions in brain areas responsible for cognitive control. Studies by Keane & Kiran (2015) and Goral et al. (2019) highlight that aphasia severity and language proficiency influence these control deficits.

4. **Lexical-Semantic Impairment**: Research on lexical-semantic impairment explores differences between L1 and L2 post-ABI. Models like the Revised Hierarchical Model (RHM) and the Distributed Feature Model describe how word retrieval processes are affected by aphasia, with errors categorized based on different loci of damage during processing.

Overall, these studies emphasize the complexity of bilingual aphasia and the need to consider pre-existing language skills and usage patterns in treatment and recovery.

The text discusses the complexities of second language acquisition and neurolinguistics with a focus on bilingual aphasia, particularly Spanish-English bilinguals. Here are the main ideas:

1. **Error Patterns in Bilingual Aphasia**: In studies involving naming tasks for individuals with bilingual aphasia, different error patterns emerged between their first (L1) and second languages (L2). For instance, in L2 English, participants often resorted to circumlocutions or used correct responses from their non-target language. In contrast, in L1 Spanish, errors like no response, semantic paraphasias, and neologisms were more common. These patterns indicate different strategies for lexical retrieval between languages.

2. **Treatment Outcomes**: Recent research has examined how treatment affects naming errors over time in bilingual individuals with aphasia. Preliminary results show that as participants improve in the treated language, they make fewer severe semantic errors and more manageable ones like circumlocutions. This suggests that treatments might strengthen the semantic system across both languages, improving communicative abilities even if word production is incorrect.

3. **Inhibitory Control Model**: The inhibitory control model explains how bilingual individuals manage lexical candidates from two languages, highlighting the role of language control in managing competing alternatives. Increased proficiency in a second language can reduce reliance on inhibitory control and make lexical retrieval more automatic.

4. **Language and Semantic Executive Control**: Bilinguals use both language control (managing task schemas) and semantic executive control (activating or inhibiting specific lexical candidates) to navigate their dual-language systems. Recent studies indicate that domain-general cognitive control, which involves broader mental processes like response selection and inhibition, also plays a role in bilingual lexical retrieval.

5. **Challenges for Bilingual Aphasia Patients**: Bilingual aphasia patients (BPWA) struggle with managing the increased demands on language and semantic executive control. Studies show variable interactions between verbal fluency tasks and cognitive control measures in BPWA, suggesting that heightened language control demands can impede semantic executive control. Additionally, language experience metrics are significant predictors of performance in BPWA, indicating a relationship between past language use and current control abilities.

Overall, the text highlights the nuanced interplay between different types of cognitive control in bilingual individuals, especially those with aphasia, emphasizing how treatment and experience shape lexical retrieval and communication strategies across languages.

Models like DISLEX incorporate aspects of bilingual lexical access, allowing simulations that predict and guide effective treatment strategies tailored to specific patient profiles.

These methods not only simulate outcomes but can also inform personalized therapeutic decisions, incorporating both clinical expertise and client preferences, as illustrated in the case study of Ruben, a stroke survivor.
